What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for the past year I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results, and no matter how many doctors or family members tell me the numbers are fine, I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw and it’s wearing me down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. [Name], it's... it's a long story, but I'm here because, well, for the past year, almost exactly a year now, I've been trying to prove something that I know is happening, and it's just completely exhausting me. I work as a phlebotomist, so I know how labs work, I know the system, I draw blood all day, I see the orders, I see the results. And... someone in the clinic system, I don't know exactly who yet, but someone is deliberately changing or holding back parts of my dad's lab results. 

I was looking at his chart, this was back in November of last year, and I saw the raw data, and then when the official report came out, it was different. Values were altered. And I've tried to explain this to his doctors, to my family, to everyone. I've printed out the screenshots, I've highlighted the discrepancies, I've shown them how the reference ranges don't align with what was initially inputted, but they just look at me like I'm crazy. They keep telling me the numbers are fine, that his kidney function is stable, that I'm just stressed. But I know what I saw. I know the chart doesn't match the original data. 

And it's just... it's wearing me down. I spend hours every night going over the portal, cross-referencing timestamps, trying to find the digital footprint of whoever is doing this, because why would they alter his labs? What are they trying to hide? Are they covering up a mistake? Are they experimenting with his treatment? Nobody will listen to me, and I just... I can't let it go until I find out the truth, but it's taking over my life.
=====================================

No, no, not at all. I mean, it's not like I think there's some huge conspiracy against me personally, or like people are following me on the street or tapping my phone. Nothing like that. I'm just a normal person. I go to work, I do my draws, I get my coffee, I go home. My coworkers and I get along fine, my supervisor is actually really great, for the most part. I don't have any issues with my neighbors or, you know, the government or anything crazy. 

It's literally just this one, very specific thing with my dad's medical records. And honestly, doctor, that's exactly what makes it so incredibly frustrating when my family looks at me like I'm losing my mind. If I were just making things up, wouldn't I be seeing problems everywhere? Wouldn't I think the whole hospital was out to get me? But I don't. I know exactly where the problem is and where it isn't. It's isolated entirely to the LIS—the Laboratory Information System—specifically regarding his renal panels starting from that second week in November. 

I'm not saying there's a hit out on me or that anyone wants to hurt me. I'm just saying someone, whoever had access to the interface that Tuesday afternoon, changed the creatinine and BUN values before the final verification step. I just want to know why they did that one specific thing. So no, nobody else is trying to harm me, and nobody is working against me in my daily life. It's just... I just need to fix this one chart issue, you know? Because the data is right there, and I just can't focus on anything else until someone acknowledges that those specific numbers were altered. But it's just his chart. It's not about me.

Well, see, that's the thing. That is exactly the thing that my family keeps throwing in my face, and it is so unbelievably frustrating. Because... no, I don't have a screenshot of the raw data from that exact split second. I am working from my memory of the preliminary numbers, yes. But you have to understand how the system works. 

When you're looking at the preliminary data, it's just there on the screen, and you don't think—nobody thinks—"Oh, I better take a picture of this right now before someone goes in and deliberately alters it." You just don't think like that! I was just checking his portal, just looking at the screen, and I saw the raw values. And I know what I saw. I stare at these kinds of panels all day long, five days a week. My brain is trained to recognize these patterns, I know what I'm looking at. So I saw the raw data, and then the page refreshed when the official, verified report was pushed through, and the numbers were different. The values had been changed. 

And because I didn't hit the print-screen button in that tiny, tiny window of time before the refresh, everyone acts like I just imagined the whole thing. Which is just... it's insulting, honestly. I have screenshots of the *aftermath*, of course. I have printouts of the final report, and I've highlighted all the discrepancies and the reference ranges that don't line up with the altered numbers, like I told you. I have a whole folder of that stuff. But the actual raw data from that Tuesday afternoon? No. I didn't print it. Because why would I? You trust the system. You trust that the numbers generated by the analyzer are the numbers that go into the final chart. 

If I had a time machine, obviously I would go back and take a picture of the monitor. I'd record the whole screen on my phone. But I didn't know I was going to need proof. I didn't know someone was going to tamper with it. And now I'm just stuck trying to explain to people that my memory of those numbers is perfectly accurate, but they just roll their eyes or tell me I'm stressed. It's like, if you don't have a piece of paper proving what was on the screen for those three minutes, your brain doesn't count anymore. But I know what I saw, Doctor. I know it. I just wish I had taken that one picture so I wouldn't have to spend every night trying to prove it.

Honestly, Doctor... if they did that? If they brought in some hospital administrator or a top-tier IT specialist and sat me down in a little conference room and pulled up the backend of the LIS to show me this supposedly pristine audit log... I would know right then and there that the log had been scrubbed. I mean, you really have to think about the logistics of how these networks operate. Anyone who has the high-level administrative clearance to go into the system and alter a preliminary lab value before the final verification is pushed through—they absolutely have the security clearance to wipe the digital footprint. That’s just basic database architecture. You don’t leave a breadcrumb trail if you’re deliberately trying to hide something. 

And this... this right here is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for the past year, this entire past year, I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it is an absolute nightmare trying to fight a system that controls its own evidence. No matter how many doctors pat me on the arm and tell me the numbers are fine, or how many of my own family members sigh and tell me I need to drop it and that his renal function is perfectly stable... I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know it. 

I draw blood, I run these things, I look at these exact same panels every single day of my professional life. I know what a critical value looks like versus a normal one. I didn't just imagine a completely different set of numbers. So no, I wouldn't doubt my memory for a single second. I would just realize that the tampering goes deeper than just the front-end user interface. And honestly, just thinking about that, thinking about having to find a way to prove that an audit trail itself was manipulated... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, utterly wearing me down to the point where I can barely sleep. But I can't just pretend I didn't see what I saw just because someone with a computer degree shows me a fake log. I just can't do it.

And... someone in the clinic system, I don't know exactly who yet, but someone is deliberately changing or holding back parts of my dad's lab results. \n\nI was looking at his chart, this was back in November of last year, and I saw the raw data, and then when the official report came out, it was different. Values were altered. And I've tried to explain this to his doctors, to my family, to everyone. I've printed out the screenshots, I've highlighted the discrepancies, I've shown them how the reference ranges don't align with what was initially inputted, but they just look at me like I'm crazy. They keep telling me the numbers are fine, that his kidney function is stable, that I'm just stressed. But I know what I saw. I know the chart doesn't match the original data. \n\nAnd it's just... it's wearing me down. I spend hours every night going over the portal, cross-referencing timestamps, trying to find the digital footprint of whoever is doing this, because why would they alter his labs? What are they trying to hide? Are they covering up a mistake? Are they experimenting with his treatment? Nobody will listen to me, and I just... I can't let it go until I find out the truth, but it's taking over my life.\n\nDoctor: Beyond the lab result issue with your dad, do you feel like there are other people or groups who are working against you or trying to harm you in other ways?\n\nPatient: No, no, not at all. I mean, it's not like I think there's some huge conspiracy against me personally, or like people are following me on the street or tapping my phone. Nothing like that. I'm just a normal person. I go to work, I do my draws, I get my coffee, I go home. My coworkers and I get along fine, my supervisor is actually really great, for the most part. I don't have any issues with my neighbors or, you know, the government or anything crazy. \n\nIt's literally just this one, very specific thing with my dad's medical records. And honestly, doctor, that's exactly what makes it so incredibly frustrating when my family looks at me like I'm losing my mind. If I were just making things up, wouldn't I be seeing problems everywhere? Wouldn't I think the whole hospital was out to get me? But I don't. I know exactly where the problem is and where it isn't. It's isolated entirely to the LIS—the Laboratory Information System—specifically regarding his renal panels starting from that second week in November. \n\nI'm not saying there's a hit out on me or that anyone wants to hurt me. I'm just saying someone, whoever had access to the interface that Tuesday afternoon, changed the creatinine and BUN values before the final verification step. I just want to know why they did that one specific thing. So no, nobody else is trying to harm me, and nobody is working against me in my daily life. It's just... I just need to fix this one chart issue, you know? Because the data is right there, and I just can't focus on anything else until someone acknowledges that those specific numbers were altered. But it's just his chart. It's not about me.\n\nDoctor: When you looked at your dad's chart back in November and saw what you thought were the original raw data values, did you take screenshots or print those pages out at that time, or are you working from your memory of what you saw?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That is exactly the thing that my family keeps throwing in my face, and it is so unbelievably frustrating. Because... no, I don't have a screenshot of the raw data from that exact split second. I am working from my memory of the preliminary numbers, yes. But you have to understand how the system works. \n\nWhen you're looking at the preliminary data, it's just there on the screen, and you don't think—nobody thinks—\"Oh, I better take a picture of this right now before someone goes in and deliberately alters it.\" You just don't think like that! I was just checking his portal, just looking at the screen, and I saw the raw values. And I know what I saw. I stare at these kinds of panels all day long, five days a week. My brain is trained to recognize these patterns, I know what I'm looking at. So I saw the raw data, and then the page refreshed when the official, verified report was pushed through, and the numbers were different. The values had been changed. \n\nAnd because I didn't hit the print-screen button in that tiny, tiny window of time before the refresh, everyone acts like I just imagined the whole thing. Which is just... it's insulting, honestly. I have screenshots of the *aftermath*, of course. I have printouts of the final report, and I've highlighted all the discrepancies and the reference ranges that don't line up with the altered numbers, like I told you. I have a whole folder of that stuff. But the actual raw data from that Tuesday afternoon? No. I didn't print it. Because why would I? You trust the system. You trust that the numbers generated by the analyzer are the numbers that go into the final chart. \n\nIf I had a time machine, obviously I would go back and take a picture of the monitor. I'd record the whole screen on my phone. But I didn't know I was going to need proof. I didn't know someone was going to tamper with it. And now I'm just stuck trying to explain to people that my memory of those numbers is perfectly accurate, but they just roll their eyes or tell me I'm stressed. It's like, if you don't have a piece of paper proving what was on the screen for those three minutes, your brain doesn't count anymore. But I know what I saw, Doctor. I know it. I just wish I had taken that one picture so I wouldn't have to spend every night trying to prove it.\n\nDoctor: If you were to look at your dad's chart again today with a hospital administrator or IT specialist who could show you the complete audit log and digital history, and they showed you that there was no alteration—that the numbers you remember seeing were actually the same as the final report—how would you interpret that? Would you consider that as evidence that maybe your memory of those preliminary numbers was inaccurate, or would you think the audit log itself had been tampered with?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor... if they did that? If they brought in some hospital administrator or a top-tier IT specialist and sat me down in a little conference room and pulled up the backend of the LIS to show me this supposedly pristine audit log... I would know right then and there that the log had been scrubbed. I mean, you really have to think about the logistics of how these networks operate. Anyone who has the high-level administrative clearance to go into the system and alter a preliminary lab value before the final verification is pushed through—they absolutely have the security clearance to wipe the digital footprint. That’s just basic database architecture. You don’t leave a breadcrumb trail if you’re deliberately trying to hide something. \n\nAnd this... this right here is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for the past year, this entire past year, I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it is an absolute nightmare trying to fight a system that controls its own evidence. No matter how many doctors pat me on the arm and tell me the numbers are fine, or how many of my own family members sigh and tell me I need to drop it and that his renal function is perfectly stable... I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know it. \n\nI draw blood, I run these things, I look at these exact same panels every single day of my professional life. I know what a critical value looks like versus a normal one. I didn't just imagine a completely different set of numbers. So no, I wouldn't doubt my memory for a single second. I would just realize that the tampering goes deeper than just the front-end user interface. And honestly, just thinking about that, thinking about having to find a way to prove that an audit trail itself was manipulated... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, utterly wearing me down to the point where I can barely sleep. But I can't just pretend I didn't see what I saw just because someone with a computer degree shows me a fake log. I just can't do it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

It's not about me.\n\nDoctor: When you looked at your dad's chart back in November and saw what you thought were the original raw data values, did you take screenshots or print those pages out at that time, or are you working from your memory of what you saw?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That is exactly the thing that my family keeps throwing in my face, and it is so unbelievably frustrating. Because... no, I don't have a screenshot of the raw data from that exact split second. I am working from my memory of the preliminary numbers, yes. But you have to understand how the system works. \n\nWhen you're looking at the preliminary data, it's just there on the screen, and you don't think—nobody thinks—\"Oh, I better take a picture of this right now before someone goes in and deliberately alters it.\" You just don't think like that! I was just checking his portal, just looking at the screen, and I saw the raw values. And I know what I saw. I stare at these kinds of panels all day long, five days a week. My brain is trained to recognize these patterns, I know what I'm looking at. So I saw the raw data, and then the page refreshed when the official, verified report was pushed through, and the numbers were different. The values had been changed. \n\nAnd because I didn't hit the print-screen button in that tiny, tiny window of time before the refresh, everyone acts like I just imagined the whole thing. Which is just... it's insulting, honestly. I have screenshots of the *aftermath*, of course. I have printouts of the final report, and I've highlighted all the discrepancies and the reference ranges that don't line up with the altered numbers, like I told you. I have a whole folder of that stuff. But the actual raw data from that Tuesday afternoon? No. I didn't print it. Because why would I? You trust the system. You trust that the numbers generated by the analyzer are the numbers that go into the final chart. \n\nIf I had a time machine, obviously I would go back and take a picture of the monitor. I'd record the whole screen on my phone. But I didn't know I was going to need proof. I didn't know someone was going to tamper with it. And now I'm just stuck trying to explain to people that my memory of those numbers is perfectly accurate, but they just roll their eyes or tell me I'm stressed. It's like, if you don't have a piece of paper proving what was on the screen for those three minutes, your brain doesn't count anymore. But I know what I saw, Doctor. I know it. I just wish I had taken that one picture so I wouldn't have to spend every night trying to prove it.\n\nDoctor: If you were to look at your dad's chart again today with a hospital administrator or IT specialist who could show you the complete audit log and digital history, and they showed you that there was no alteration—that the numbers you remember seeing were actually the same as the final report—how would you interpret that? Would you consider that as evidence that maybe your memory of those preliminary numbers was inaccurate, or would you think the audit log itself had been tampered with?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor... if they did that? If they brought in some hospital administrator or a top-tier IT specialist and sat me down in a little conference room and pulled up the backend of the LIS to show me this supposedly pristine audit log... I would know right then and there that the log had been scrubbed. I mean, you really have to think about the logistics of how these networks operate. Anyone who has the high-level administrative clearance to go into the system and alter a preliminary lab value before the final verification is pushed through—they absolutely have the security clearance to wipe the digital footprint. That’s just basic database architecture. You don’t leave a breadcrumb trail if you’re deliberately trying to hide something. \n\nAnd this... this right here is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for the past year, this entire past year, I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it is an absolute nightmare trying to fight a system that controls its own evidence. No matter how many doctors pat me on the arm and tell me the numbers are fine, or how many of my own family members sigh and tell me I need to drop it and that his renal function is perfectly stable... I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know it. \n\nI draw blood, I run these things, I look at these exact same panels every single day of my professional life. I know what a critical value looks like versus a normal one. I didn't just imagine a completely different set of numbers. So no, I wouldn't doubt my memory for a single second. I would just realize that the tampering goes deeper than just the front-end user interface. And honestly, just thinking about that, thinking about having to find a way to prove that an audit trail itself was manipulated... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, utterly wearing me down to the point where I can barely sleep. But I can't just pretend I didn't see what I saw just because someone with a computer degree shows me a fake log. I just can't do it.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned this has been going on for almost exactly a year now. Over this past year, have you been able to maintain your job as a phlebotomist without significant problems, or has this preoccupation with your dad's lab results started affecting your work performance, your ability to focus on your regular duties, or your relationships with coworkers?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 33 / phlebotomist / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Outside of the dispute about my dad’s records, I still keep my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself, and manage normal day-to-day responsibilities.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, see, that's the thing. That's the part that really proves I'm not... you know, that I'm not just losing my grip on reality, like my sister likes to imply. Because, yes, it's been a year. Twelve months of this absolute, grinding frustration. But through all of it? Every single day of it? I still get up, I put on my scrubs, and I go to work. I haven't missed a single shift, Doctor. Not one. I still do my draws, and I'm good at it. I have some of the toughest sticks in the clinic, patients who always ask for me specifically because I can find a vein when nobody else can. I still manage my whole life. I pay my rent on time, my car payment is up to date, my credit score hasn't dropped a single point. I go grocery shopping, I meal prep on Sundays... I take care of myself. I really do. 

And at work, it's not like I'm sitting in the breakroom obsessing over this out loud or cornering my coworkers to talk about my dad's creatinine levels. I know how to keep it separate. I mean, sure, maybe I'm a little quieter sometimes, or maybe I don't always want to go out for drinks after a Friday shift because I'm just so exhausted from thinking about this chart issue all night, but my actual performance? My ability to focus on the patient in front of me, tie the tourniquet, prep the site, draw the vials, and label them correctly? That hasn't slipped at all. My supervisor just gave me a perfectly fine annual review last month. 

So it's not like my whole life is falling apart. It's just... it's just this one, specific, incredibly stressful thing that occupies the space in my head when I'm not actively doing something else. When I'm at work, I'm working. When I'm paying bills, I'm paying bills. But the second I have a quiet moment, the second I'm just sitting in my car before I go into my apartment, or lying in bed trying to sleep... that's when it all comes back. That's when I start running through the numbers again, trying to figure out how to prove what I saw. But no, to answer your question, outside of the dispute about my dad’s records, I still keep my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself, and manage normal day-to-day responsibilities. I'm fully functional. I just need this one thing fixed.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, no, it's... I mean, I don't think there's any sort of magic trick to it or anything bizarre, if that's what you're asking. It's exactly what you just said first. They just logged in. 

People outside the medical field, they tend to think these electronic health record systems are these impenetrable, high-security fortresses, but they really aren't. I mean, I use these systems every single day. I've worked with Epic, Cerner, Sunquest, you name it. I know exactly what the interface looks like on the backend. There is literally just a little field, a standard text box on the screen where the raw analyzer data populates in the queue before the lab director or the attending physician actually signs off and finalizes the batch. 

So it’s literally just a matter of someone—someone who has the right administrative credentials, like a Level 4 or Level 5 access—just sitting at a keyboard. They could be in a back office at the clinic, or honestly, they could be sitting on their couch at home using a hospital VPN, because everyone has remote access these days. And they just... they just click the box. They highlight the real number, they hit backspace, and they type in a new one. It takes maybe five seconds. Five seconds to completely alter a patient's medical history. 

And that is the part that just drives me absolutely crazy, because it's so mundane! It's not some elaborate, crazy hacker scenario from a movie where alarms are going off. It's just a person at a regular computer monitor, probably drinking their morning coffee, manually typing over the real data before they hit 'verify' and push it through. And because they have the security clearance, the system just accepts it. It doesn't throw up a red flag to the patient, it just pushes the fake, sanitized number straight to the patient portal like nothing ever happened. 

So, no, there isn't anything else to it. I don't have some wild, complicated theory about how the numbers changed. It's just basic, boring data entry by someone who needed to hide the truth about his real lab values. That's what makes it so incredibly insidious, Doctor. It’s just so incredibly easy to do if you have the password, and they know that nobody is ever going to question it because the computer says it's official.

But it's just his chart. It's not about me.\n\nDoctor: When you looked at your dad's chart back in November and saw what you thought were the original raw data values, did you take screenshots or print those pages out at that time, or are you working from your memory of what you saw?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That is exactly the thing that my family keeps throwing in my face, and it is so unbelievably frustrating. Because... no, I don't have a screenshot of the raw data from that exact split second. I am working from my memory of the preliminary numbers, yes. But you have to understand how the system works. \n\nWhen you're looking at the preliminary data, it's just there on the screen, and you don't think—nobody thinks—\"Oh, I better take a picture of this right now before someone goes in and deliberately alters it.\" You just don't think like that! I was just checking his portal, just looking at the screen, and I saw the raw values. And I know what I saw. I stare at these kinds of panels all day long, five days a week. My brain is trained to recognize these patterns, I know what I'm looking at. So I saw the raw data, and then the page refreshed when the official, verified report was pushed through, and the numbers were different. The values had been changed. \n\nAnd because I didn't hit the print-screen button in that tiny, tiny window of time before the refresh, everyone acts like I just imagined the whole thing. Which is just... it's insulting, honestly. I have screenshots of the *aftermath*, of course. I have printouts of the final report, and I've highlighted all the discrepancies and the reference ranges that don't line up with the altered numbers, like I told you. I have a whole folder of that stuff. But the actual raw data from that Tuesday afternoon? No. I didn't print it. Because why would I? You trust the system. You trust that the numbers generated by the analyzer are the numbers that go into the final chart. \n\nIf I had a time machine, obviously I would go back and take a picture of the monitor. I'd record the whole screen on my phone. But I didn't know I was going to need proof. I didn't know someone was going to tamper with it. And now I'm just stuck trying to explain to people that my memory of those numbers is perfectly accurate, but they just roll their eyes or tell me I'm stressed. It's like, if you don't have a piece of paper proving what was on the screen for those three minutes, your brain doesn't count anymore. But I know what I saw, Doctor. I know it. I just wish I had taken that one picture so I wouldn't have to spend every night trying to prove it.\n\nDoctor: If you were to look at your dad's chart again today with a hospital administrator or IT specialist who could show you the complete audit log and digital history, and they showed you that there was no alteration—that the numbers you remember seeing were actually the same as the final report—how would you interpret that? Would you consider that as evidence that maybe your memory of those preliminary numbers was inaccurate, or would you think the audit log itself had been tampered with?\n\nPatient: Honestly, Doctor... if they did that? If they brought in some hospital administrator or a top-tier IT specialist and sat me down in a little conference room and pulled up the backend of the LIS to show me this supposedly pristine audit log... I would know right then and there that the log had been scrubbed. I mean, you really have to think about the logistics of how these networks operate. Anyone who has the high-level administrative clearance to go into the system and alter a preliminary lab value before the final verification is pushed through—they absolutely have the security clearance to wipe the digital footprint. That’s just basic database architecture. You don’t leave a breadcrumb trail if you’re deliberately trying to hide something. \n\nAnd this... this right here is exactly why I’m here talking to you, because for the past year, this entire past year, I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it is an absolute nightmare trying to fight a system that controls its own evidence. No matter how many doctors pat me on the arm and tell me the numbers are fine, or how many of my own family members sigh and tell me I need to drop it and that his renal function is perfectly stable... I know the chart doesn’t match what I saw. I know it. \n\nI draw blood, I run these things, I look at these exact same panels every single day of my professional life. I know what a critical value looks like versus a normal one. I didn't just imagine a completely different set of numbers. So no, I wouldn't doubt my memory for a single second. I would just realize that the tampering goes deeper than just the front-end user interface. And honestly, just thinking about that, thinking about having to find a way to prove that an audit trail itself was manipulated... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, utterly wearing me down to the point where I can barely sleep. But I can't just pretend I didn't see what I saw just because someone with a computer degree shows me a fake log. I just can't do it.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this has been going on for almost exactly a year now. Over this past year, have you been able to maintain your job as a phlebotomist without significant problems, or has this preoccupation with your dad's lab results started affecting your work performance, your ability to focus on your regular duties, or your relationships with coworkers?\n\nPatient: Well, see, that's the thing. That's the part that really proves I'm not... you know, that I'm not just losing my grip on reality, like my sister likes to imply. Because, yes, it's been a year. Twelve months of this absolute, grinding frustration. But through all of it? Every single day of it? I still get up, I put on my scrubs, and I go to work. I haven't missed a single shift, Doctor. Not one. I still do my draws, and I'm good at it. I have some of the toughest sticks in the clinic, patients who always ask for me specifically because I can find a vein when nobody else can. I still manage my whole life. I pay my rent on time, my car payment is up to date, my credit score hasn't dropped a single point. I go grocery shopping, I meal prep on Sundays... I take care of myself. I really do. \n\nAnd at work, it's not like I'm sitting in the breakroom obsessing over this out loud or cornering my coworkers to talk about my dad's creatinine levels. I know how to keep it separate. I mean, sure, maybe I'm a little quieter sometimes, or maybe I don't always want to go out for drinks after a Friday shift because I'm just so exhausted from thinking about this chart issue all night, but my actual performance? My ability to focus on the patient in front of me, tie the tourniquet, prep the site, draw the vials, and label them correctly? That hasn't slipped at all. My supervisor just gave me a perfectly fine annual review last month. \n\nSo it's not like my whole life is falling apart. It's just... it's just this one, specific, incredibly stressful thing that occupies the space in my head when I'm not actively doing something else. When I'm at work, I'm working. When I'm paying bills, I'm paying bills. But the second I have a quiet moment, the second I'm just sitting in my car before I go into my apartment, or lying in bed trying to sleep... that's when it all comes back. That's when I start running through the numbers again, trying to figure out how to prove what I saw. But no, to answer your question, outside of the dispute about my dad’s records, I still keep my phlebotomy shifts, pay my bills, take care of myself, and manage normal day-to-day responsibilities. I'm fully functional. I just need this one thing fixed.\n\nDoctor: When you think about someone changing those lab values in the system, do you believe this person physically altered the numbers using their computer access—like, they logged in and manually changed the values before the report was finalized—or is there something else about how you think it happened that seems different from that?\n\nPatient: Well, no, it's... I mean, I don't think there's any sort of magic trick to it or anything bizarre, if that's what you're asking. It's exactly what you just said first. They just logged in. \n\nPeople outside the medical field, they tend to think these electronic health record systems are these impenetrable, high-security fortresses, but they really aren't. I mean, I use these systems every single day. I've worked with Epic, Cerner, Sunquest, you name it. I know exactly what the interface looks like on the backend. There is literally just a little field, a standard text box on the screen where the raw analyzer data populates in the queue before the lab director or the attending physician actually signs off and finalizes the batch. \n\nSo it’s literally just a matter of someone—someone who has the right administrative credentials, like a Level 4 or Level 5 access—just sitting at a keyboard. They could be in a back office at the clinic, or honestly, they could be sitting on their couch at home using a hospital VPN, because everyone has remote access these days. And they just... they just click the box. They highlight the real number, they hit backspace, and they type in a new one. It takes maybe five seconds. Five seconds to completely alter a patient's medical history. \n\nAnd that is the part that just drives me absolutely crazy, because it's so mundane! It's not some elaborate, crazy hacker scenario from a movie where alarms are going off. It's just a person at a regular computer monitor, probably drinking their morning coffee, manually typing over the real data before they hit 'verify' and push it through. And because they have the security clearance, the system just accepts it. It doesn't throw up a red flag to the patient, it just pushes the fake, sanitized number straight to the patient portal like nothing ever happened. \n\nSo, no, there isn't anything else to it. I don't have some wild, complicated theory about how the numbers changed. It's just basic, boring data entry by someone who needed to hide the truth about his real lab values. That's what makes it so incredibly insidious, Doctor. It’s just so incredibly easy to do if you have the password, and they know that nobody is ever going to question it because the computer says it's official.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

No, no, absolutely not. I know exactly what you're getting at with that question, Doctor. You're asking if I'm, you know, seeing things that aren't there or hearing voices or something like that. And my hearing is perfectly fine, too. I don't hear whispers, I don't see shadows, I don't have any strange sensations at all. 

And honestly, that's why it's so incredibly frustrating when people—especially my sister—look at me with that pitying expression, like I've completely lost touch with reality. They treat me like someone who is standing on a street corner talking to a wall, when all I am doing is reporting exactly what I read on a standard, high-definition computer monitor. There is a huge, massive difference between imagining things out of thin air and reading a literal string of data on a screen. I mean, think about it... if I were prone to seeing things that weren't there, wouldn't I be seeing weird things everywhere? Wouldn't I be messing up my blood draws at work? Wouldn't I be misreading patient wristbands or mislabeling vials? But I just told you, my performance at work is flawless. 

The only thing—the *only* thing—that is in dispute is what I saw on that specific screen that day in November. And I didn't imagine those numbers. I read them. Just like I read the labels on my blood tubes every single day, just like I read the grocery list, just like I read my own mail. So no, I don't have any of those unusual sensations you're asking about. I don't see or hear anything that isn't real. I just have a very, very clear memory of a very real lab value that someone else decided to type over, and I'm not going to let anyone convince me it was just my imagination.

You're trying to figure out if I'm imagining things in my day-to-day life. But no, nothing like that. Never. I have absolutely perfect vision—20/20, actually, which you really need when you're a phlebotomist. Sometimes you're dealing with these tiny, rolling, fragile veins on elderly patients, or you're doing a tricky pediatric draw, and if your eyesight isn't incredibly sharp, you're going to blow the vein and cause a hematoma. And my hearing is perfectly fine, too. I don't hear whispers, I don't see shadows, I don't have any strange sensations at all. \n\nAnd honestly, that's why it's so incredibly frustrating when people—especially my sister—look at me with that pitying expression, like I've completely lost touch with reality. They treat me like someone who is standing on a street corner talking to a wall, when all I am doing is reporting exactly what I read on a standard, high-definition computer monitor. There is a huge, massive difference between imagining things out of thin air and reading a literal string of data on a screen. I mean, think about it... if I were prone to seeing things that weren't there, wouldn't I be seeing weird things everywhere? Wouldn't I be messing up my blood draws at work? Wouldn't I be misreading patient wristbands or mislabeling vials? But I just told you, my performance at work is flawless. \n\nThe only thing—the *only* thing—that is in dispute is what I saw on that specific screen that day in November. So no, I don't have any of those unusual sensations you're asking about. I don't see or hear anything that isn't real. Well, honestly, whether it's one person or five people... I don't even know anymore. Sometimes I look at the timestamps and I think, okay, maybe it's just one rogue admin covering their tracks. But then I think about how smoothly it was done, and I wonder if maybe there's a supervisor who signed off on it, or... but see, that's not even the main issue. That's not why I'm so exhausted. 

The reality is, I’m here, sitting in this office with you right now, because for the past year—twelve entire months of my life, every single day—I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And it shouldn't be this hard! I have tried everything. I've printed out the portal pages, I've tracked the time the batch was finalized versus when the notification came through, I've brought all of this to his primary care doctor, I've brought it to the specialist... I even sat my sister down at my kitchen table and pointed right at the discrepancies with a highlighter. 

But it's like I'm speaking a completely different language to these people. No matter how many doctors look me in the eye and patronize me and tell me the numbers are fine, or how many family members tell me I need to just let it go and trust the system... I can't. I just can't, because I know the chart they are looking at right now does not match what I saw on that screen. I know it. I have a photographic memory for these things, especially when it comes to my own father's health. 

And trying to get anyone to actually look at the evidence, trying to trace exactly who clicked what and when... it’s just wearing me down. It is so utterly, completely exhausting. I mean, you try waking up every single morning knowing a medical record has been sanitized and nobody believes you. You try looking your own sister in the face while she tells you the doctors know best, when you know for an absolute fact that those doctors are making decisions based on fake data. So, one person, multiple people... it doesn't even matter at this point. What matters is that they are hiding his real results, and I am just so tired of being the only one fighting to expose it.

No, I mean... I really don't know why. I honestly don't. And that is exactly what my sister always throws in my face. She always asks me what the motive is, as if I am supposed to have this whole grand conspiracy mapped out with red string on a corkboard. But I don't! I don't have any theories about why someone would do this. I have laid awake at night, staring at the ceiling at two in the morning, racking my brain, trying to figure out if it was an insurance thing, or if someone made a mistake and just panicked and tried to cover their tracks, or... I just don't know. I have absolutely no idea what the motive is. 

And as for his doctors, or the hospital administrators, or the whole IT department... no, I don't think they are all sitting around a big conference table plotting against my dad. I really don't. His primary care doctor is a perfectly nice man who probably sees forty patients a day. He is incredibly busy. When he opens my dad's chart, he is just looking at the screen, trusting whatever sanitized number the system feeds him, and then he moves on to the next room. He doesn't know the data is bad. The administrators probably don't know either. I am not trying to accuse the entire hospital of being in on some massive, evil cover-up, because that would be crazy. 

I am just a phlebotomist. At work, if a blood tube gets mislabeled, I don't sit there and try to psychoanalyze the person who printed the wrong sticker. I don't assume the whole pathology department is involved in a plot to mislabel that specific tube. I just look at it and say, hey, this label is wrong, the data doesn't match the patient, and we need to fix it. That is all I am doing here! But with my dad, because I can't hand everyone a perfectly wrapped-up explanation for exactly who pressed the backspace key and what their deep, dark secret motive was, everyone just dismisses the fact that it happened at all. 

It is so incredibly frustrating, Doctor. Just because I don't know the why doesn't mean I am wrong about the what. I don't need to know why a car ran a red light to know that it ran a red light, you know what I mean? I saw the real numbers before they were changed. I know what I saw. I don't have some wild theory about a hospital-wide conspiracy, and I don't know why that one person decided to alter the record. I just know that they did, and it drives me absolutely insane that nobody will take it seriously just because I can't explain the motive.

I don't have any theories about why someone would do this. I have laid awake at night, staring at the ceiling at two in the morning, racking my brain, trying to figure out if it was an insurance thing, or if someone made a mistake and just panicked and tried to cover their tracks, or... I just don't know. I have absolutely no idea what the motive is. \n\nAnd as for his doctors, or the hospital administrators, or the whole IT department... no, I don't think they are all sitting around a big conference table plotting against my dad. I really don't. His primary care doctor is a perfectly nice man who probably sees forty patients a day. He is incredibly busy. When he opens my dad's chart, he is just looking at the screen, trusting whatever sanitized number the system feeds him, and then he moves on to the next room. He doesn't know the data is bad. The administrators probably don't know either. I am not trying to accuse the entire hospital of being in on some massive, evil cover-up, because that would be crazy. \n\nI am just a phlebotomist. At work, if a blood tube gets mislabeled, I don't sit there and try to psychoanalyze the person who printed the wrong sticker. I don't assume the whole pathology department is involved in a plot to mislabel that specific tube. I just look at it and say, hey, this label is wrong, the data doesn't match the patient, and we need to fix it. That is all I am doing here! But with my dad, because I can't hand everyone a perfectly wrapped-up explanation for exactly who pressed the backspace key and what their deep, dark secret motive was, everyone just dismisses the fact that it happened at all. \n\nIt is so incredibly frustrating, Doctor. Just because I don't know the why doesn't mean I am wrong about the what. I don't need to know why a car ran a red light to know that it ran a red light, you know what I mean? I saw the real numbers before they were changed. I know what I saw. I don't have some wild theory about a hospital-wide conspiracy, and I don't know why that one person decided to alter the record. I just know that they did, and it drives me absolutely insane that nobody will take it seriously just because I can't explain the motive.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Hypothetically? I mean... I just... I can't even entertain that, Doctor. I really can't. It feels absolutely, one hundred percent impossible. Because you have to understand how my brain works, and how my training works. When you draw blood for a living, when you are handling patient samples day in and day out, you are trained—drilled, really—to look at numbers and names and values exactly as they are. You don't just glance and guess. You don't let your brain "fill in" the blanks, because if you do that, somebody gets hurt. I look at data for a living. I know how to read a screen. 

So when you ask if it's possible that my memory is just slightly off... no. No, it isn't. And honestly, that is exactly why I am sitting here in this chair right now. I’m here because for the past year—an entire year of my life just gone to this—I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And I know I keep repeating myself, but I really need you to hear how serious this is. 

I have spent months pulling up these records, comparing the timestamps, looking at the portal, and no matter how many doctors look at the current chart and patronize me and tell me the numbers are fine, no matter how many times my own family members tell me I'm just stressed or misremembering... I know the truth. I know for an absolute, undeniable fact that the chart they are all looking at right now does not match what I saw with my own two eyes on that preliminary screen. It just doesn't. 

And having to defend my own memory, having to constantly fight this battle every single day when I know I'm right... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, thoroughly wearing me down to the bone. You ask how it sits with me to think I might be wrong? It makes my skin crawl, because I'm *not* wrong. If I were wrong, I could just go back to my life! I could just go to work, come home, watch TV, and be a normal person. I would love to be wrong! But I can't be, because I saw the real numbers. I saw them. And trying to pretend that my brain just magically made up a highly specific, clinically significant lab value just to make everyone else in my life comfortable is... well, it's just not something I can do. I won't do it. I know what I saw.

It doesn't throw up a red flag to the patient, it just pushes the fake, sanitized number straight to the patient portal like nothing ever happened. \n\nSo, no, there isn't anything else to it. I don't have some wild, complicated theory about how the numbers changed. It's just basic, boring data entry by someone who needed to hide the truth about his real lab values. That's what makes it so incredibly insidious, Doctor. It’s just so incredibly easy to do if you have the password, and they know that nobody is ever going to question it because the computer says it's official.\n\nDoctor: Besides what you saw on the computer screen that day in November, have you ever experienced seeing things, hearing things, or noticing other unusual sensations that seemed real to you at the time but that other people around you couldn't perceive or didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, no, absolutely not. I know exactly what you're getting at with that question, Doctor. You're asking if I'm, you know, seeing things that aren't there or hearing voices or something like that. You're trying to figure out if I'm imagining things in my day-to-day life. But no, nothing like that. Never. I have absolutely perfect vision—20/20, actually, which you really need when you're a phlebotomist. Sometimes you're dealing with these tiny, rolling, fragile veins on elderly patients, or you're doing a tricky pediatric draw, and if your eyesight isn't incredibly sharp, you're going to blow the vein and cause a hematoma. And my hearing is perfectly fine, too. I don't hear whispers, I don't see shadows, I don't have any strange sensations at all. \n\nAnd honestly, that's why it's so incredibly frustrating when people—especially my sister—look at me with that pitying expression, like I've completely lost touch with reality. They treat me like someone who is standing on a street corner talking to a wall, when all I am doing is reporting exactly what I read on a standard, high-definition computer monitor. There is a huge, massive difference between imagining things out of thin air and reading a literal string of data on a screen. I mean, think about it... if I were prone to seeing things that weren't there, wouldn't I be seeing weird things everywhere? Wouldn't I be messing up my blood draws at work? Wouldn't I be misreading patient wristbands or mislabeling vials? But I just told you, my performance at work is flawless. \n\nThe only thing—the *only* thing—that is in dispute is what I saw on that specific screen that day in November. And I didn't imagine those numbers. I read them. Just like I read the labels on my blood tubes every single day, just like I read the grocery list, just like I read my own mail. I just have a very, very clear memory of a very real lab value that someone else decided to type over, and I'm not going to let anyone convince me it was just my imagination.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that you've been trying to trace who altered the labs by looking at timestamps and digital footprints. I have a photographic memory for these things, especially when it comes to my own father's health. \n\nAnd trying to get anyone to actually look at the evidence, trying to trace exactly who clicked what and when... it’s just wearing me down. I saw them. I just look at it and say, hey, this label is wrong, the data doesn't match the patient, and we need to fix it. That is all I am doing here! But with my dad, because I can't hand everyone a perfectly wrapped-up explanation for exactly who pressed the backspace key and what their deep, dark secret motive was, everyone just dismisses the fact that it happened at all. \n\nIt is so incredibly frustrating, Doctor. Just because I don't know the why doesn't mean I am wrong about the what. I don't need to know why a car ran a red light to know that it ran a red light, you know what I mean? I saw the real numbers before they were changed. I know what I saw. I don't have some wild theory about a hospital-wide conspiracy, and I don't know why that one person decided to alter the record. I just know that they did, and it drives me absolutely insane that nobody will take it seriously just because I can't explain the motive.\n\nDoctor: If you were to accept, just hypothetically for a moment, that your memory of those preliminary numbers might not be perfectly accurate—that maybe your brain filled in or slightly altered what you actually saw that day—how would that possibility sit with you? Could that be true, or does it feel absolutely impossible?\n\nPatient: Hypothetically? I mean... I just... I can't even entertain that, Doctor. I really can't. It feels absolutely, one hundred percent impossible. Because you have to understand how my brain works, and how my training works. When you draw blood for a living, when you are handling patient samples day in and day out, you are trained—drilled, really—to look at numbers and names and values exactly as they are. You don't just glance and guess. You don't let your brain \"fill in\" the blanks, because if you do that, somebody gets hurt. I look at data for a living. I know how to read a screen. \n\nSo when you ask if it's possible that my memory is just slightly off... no. No, it isn't. And honestly, that is exactly why I am sitting here in this chair right now. I’m here because for the past year—an entire year of my life just gone to this—I’ve been trying to prove that someone in the clinic system is changing or holding back parts of my dad’s lab results. And I know I keep repeating myself, but I really need you to hear how serious this is. \n\nI have spent months pulling up these records, comparing the timestamps, looking at the portal, and no matter how many doctors look at the current chart and patronize me and tell me the numbers are fine, no matter how many times my own family members tell me I'm just stressed or misremembering... I know the truth. I know for an absolute, undeniable fact that the chart they are all looking at right now does not match what I saw with my own two eyes on that preliminary screen. It just doesn't. \n\nAnd having to defend my own memory, having to constantly fight this battle every single day when I know I'm right... it’s wearing me down. It is completely, thoroughly wearing me down to the bone. You ask how it sits with me to think I might be wrong? It makes my skin crawl, because I'm *not* wrong. If I were wrong, I could just go back to my life! I could just go to work, come home, watch TV, and be a normal person. I would love to be wrong! But I can't be, because I saw the real numbers. I saw them. And trying to pretend that my brain just magically made up a highly specific, clinically significant lab value just to make everyone else in my life comfortable is... well, it's just not something I can do. I won't do it. I know what I saw.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no, I don't think it's anything... like, science fiction or whatever you might be implying. I'm not talking about hackers in dark basements bypassing firewalls or anything impossible like that. It's just a regular computer system! I mean, look, I work in a clinic. I'm a phlebotomist. I know exactly how Epic and Cerner and all these Electronic Medical Record systems operate. You log in with your credentials, you do your little badge tap or you type your username and password, and you have access to the pending labs. It's not magic. It is entirely, one hundred percent real-world.

It's just someone sitting at a desk, probably under fluorescent lights, maybe sipping a stale cup of coffee, who opened my dad's chart, saw the preliminary numbers—the exact same numbers that I saw—and just... clicked the box, pressed the backspace key, typed in a new, sanitized number, and hit 'Finalize.' That's it! It is so incredibly mundane, and I think that's what makes it so terrifying, honestly. The fact that with just a few simple keystrokes, someone can completely rewrite a person's medical reality, and then the system just locks it in as the official truth. 

So no, I don't think there's any weird, unexplainable glitch or something outside of normal system capabilities. I know how these systems track revisions, which is exactly why I've been begging for the audit logs! Every EMR has an audit trail, Doctor. It tracks every single click, every time a page is viewed, every time a field is modified, down to the millisecond. If they would just pull that, they would see the real-world, totally normal computer access of whoever did this. 

But of course, getting the hospital IT department or the privacy officer to actually release a full audit trail to a patient's daughter... it's like pulling teeth. They give you the runaround, they say, 'Oh, the portal only shows finalized results,' or 'Preliminary data isn't stored that way once it's overwritten.' Which is a lie! I know it's a lie because I use these exact same systems every single day at work. I know exactly how easy it is to alter a field before the final sign-off. It's literally just a keyboard and a mouse. That's all it takes. Just someone with normal, everyday administrative access, sitting at a normal computer, deciding to change the numbers.
